　Proposition 21-14 (Corr-D excludes pure records and concomitant traces) \[proved\]  In 𝔐21 the state of R supplies direct arguments of the generating functions of these quantities: g\_P = a(u\_P)b(c\_P)/(a(u\_P) + b(c\_P)) (L1); Q\_P is a truncated rational function of g\_P (L2; strictly increasing within the working domain); load\_P = σQ\_P/Q, cap and E pass through ω = Q\_PSe, and the channel-exit S\_P = out\_P passes through load₁, cap₂, E₂ (L4, L5); Δu\_P and Δc\_P are given by the same set of components. Hence the dependence of the successors on R is a direct dependence in the generating equations, not a correlation through hidden quantities (*The General Form of Law*: “correlation is not dynamical bearing”). The witness uses the counterfactual “other positions fixed, only R changed”, and activity and write-back all change; it does not rely on a simple-deletion but-for definition (H3 is not invoked; the witness given is stronger than H3 requires). ∎ For the decomposition of the same-beat write-back see 9.2(2) (L3e): reverting the increments places the whole difference in the channel-reach write-back on the activity-mediated A branch, the D branch is 0, and after gridding a positive difference in the single-beat write increment remains.  
　Corr-Ω (ΩSupport, *The General Form of Law* Section 4; Ω\_W per Field Chapter 1 v2.7, Definition 12): the bearing segment is Q\_L = R (u\_P → c\_P, the Gully-Line γ\_P of route P); for each i, the applicable entry is the footslope node (inflow i enters directly at the node where u\_P lies), and e ⇝*\_R Q\_L is structural reachability (under the fixed topology, simply the existence of γ\_P); the three facts of Ω\_W(Q\_L, Rel(L*)): first, if an event k within the window actually passes along γ\_P (the actual Q\_P at that event beat is \> 0) and Rel(L*) holds on that event, it is recorded as a “bearing pass”; the second and later bearing passes within the window are re-traversal records (the first is only a pass, not a re-traversal), and the number of records is ≥ 2; second, Brk\_W(γ\_P) = 0: the actual conductance g\_P \> 0 at every macro-step boundary in the window and at t (conductance is checked at macro-step boundaries), and the actual Q\_P \> 0 at every event beat in the window (throughflow is checked at event beats); third, the window is divided into 2 equal segments, each containing a re-traversal record. Ω\_W belongs to the segment and is not labeled by re-entry condition (scope statement (1) of proof (iii-a) in *The Necessity Form of Law\* v1.4). Entry coverage is stated separately for B and S: the conductances of the two fixed series segments u\_P → c\_P are both positive, and at the end of macro-step 10 the actual Q\_P is positive for both inflow classes and the duty reading holds; this is the model witness of same-segment access (L4c). The segment-level result does not imply that high and low flows have each re-traversed in both halves of the window (L4d).  
　Proposition 21-15 (Ω^c ⇒ segment-level ΩSupport) \[proved\]  When Ω^c (replay\_hist\_path: for each class c, a re-traversal record must be a class-c candidate whose preceding class-c event within the window is also a candidate) equals 1, segment-level ΩSupport equals 1. Proof: a class-c re-traversal record k is a bearing pass and is preceded by a class-c bearing pass in the same window, so k is not the first bearing pass in the window and is a formal re-traversal record; ≥ 2 records for each class imply ≥ 2 formal records; records of each class covering both halves imply formal records covering both halves; the Brk conditions of the two readings are the same (conductance at macro-step boundaries, throughflow at event beats). ∎ The converse fails (L4a: the formal reading of H1 is 1 from macro-step 10 on, while the strict reading is 0 throughout). So Ω^c is a stricter sufficient reading of segment-level ΩSupport, not an equivalent of it.  
　Relation between DynamicRole^h and DynamicRole: DynamicRole^h is a historical-process witness (for any process) of \[17\] Definition 6′ on formed positions; the formal DynamicRole is the dependence of the duty's activity and write-back on the present state; the two have different objects, and no mutual inference is claimed. The formal item always uses Corr-D.  
(4) Two Kinds of Evidence for the Current Holding-Tendency, and the Semantic Condition of SC\_Hold \[verified: exhaustive decision\] \[verified\]  
　*The Necessity Form of Law* v1.4 Section 6: one of the ways of supplying SC\_Hold is to “set up a separate current-capacity quantity (declaring its input conditions, maintenance conditions, evaluation horizon and criterion, as permitted by Definition 12 of *The Mathematical Form of the Field* v2.7) and prove that it is given by the antecedents”. This paper gives two complementary current capacities, stated in separate columns; neither is inferred from the number of re-traversal passes, and SC\_Hold is not automatically discharged because of them.  
　(A) The finite-horizon capacity under perturbation, Cap\_8 = Dom\_8(P8) (the frozen protocol of 6.3; evaluation horizon N = 8; no infinite look-ahead).  
　Proposition 21-16 (Antecedents ⇒ Cap\_8) \[verified: exhaustive decision + pointwise recomputation\]  (1) On 𝓗\_10: among all {B, S} prefix nodes at t = 8, 9, 10 (256 + 512 + 1024 = 1792, counted by prefix node, not by trajectories containing repeated prefixes), Established ∧ DynamicRole ∧ ReplayHist (formal ΩSupport) holds at 63 (with pairwise distinct final states), and Cap\_8 is 1 at all of them (L5a). (2) Pointwise recomputation outside 𝓗\_10: for the 26 trajectory–time records in H1, H1m and H★ with t \> 10 (i.e., t ≥ 11) at which the antecedents hold (6 of them at macro-steps 11–16 of H1), Cap\_8 is 1 at all of them (L5c; t = 10 is covered by (1) and not counted again). (3) Intervention trajectories: the structural positive ends of the 30 intervention trajectories of L6, from the intervention time on, deduplicate to 207 states under the projection removing V, and Cap\_8 is 1 at all of them (L6f; default successor protocol, no sustained intervention). Cap\_8 is not the qualification itself: at the ends of macro-steps 4–9 of H1, Cap\_8 = 1 while ΩSupport = 0 (L5b); only “antecedents ⇒ Cap\_8” is used.  
　(B) The two-entry cycle without external maintenance (L5d, L5e). From the end of macro-step 10 of H1, continue with BS alternation and with SB alternation respectively (3 rest beats after each event, no external maintenance), with (phase, all non-V positions) as the exact key: for both initial phases the key first appears at the end of continuation macro-step 27 and appears again at the end of macro-step 29, that is, the state together with its phase repeats exactly with period 2; at every read in the prefix and in the cycle, the main relation holds for both classes. Hence there exist specific infinite continuations covering both first entries (V only keeps accounts, does not write back, and is not in the key). From the end of macro-step 15 of H1m the corresponding values are 22 and 24. Counterexample: under sustained low flow only, the next read fails after 27 continuation macro-steps from the end of macro-step 10 of H1, and after 29 continuation macro-steps from the end of macro-step 15 of H1m; the cycle capacity is limited to the listed alternating entries.  
　(C) Division of labor between the two columns: Cap\_8 proves a property “under perturbation, over a finite horizon”; the cycle proves “infinite continuation without perturbation, under specific alternating entries”. The two are complementary model evidence of the current holding-tendency. Matching the selected capacity to the current qualification content (ReplayPotential) of the source text's “holding-tendency” remains a semantic condition; it is listed as an explicit condition and is not issued (14.2).  
　Not completed: a general proof of Cap\_8 on histories with perturbations and on longer histories; generalization of the cycle to other entries and parameters.  
(5) The Read-Back Chain (Conditional Statement)  
　Theorem 21-17 (Structural positive end on the selected R, and forward read-back of Law)  Object: 𝔐21 with default parameters; L\*, I, R = {u\_P, c\_P} and W frozen per 8.5(2); h is the sediment-supply boundary; history H1.  
　(1) \[proved + verified\] The structural positive end Established ∧ DynamicRole ∧ ΩSupport of the selected R first holds at the end of macro-step 10 of H1 and is maintained for 10 ≤ t ≤ 16; at the ends of macro-steps 0–9, at least one item on this R is 0 (L1a).  
　(2) \[cited\] *The Necessity Form of Law* Theorem 4: ∃R\[Established ∧ Govern\] ⇒ Law, carrying SC\_Hold; it does not carry C0, C2, H1–H3 and does not pass through formal-layer read-back.  
　(3) \[verified\] Model evidence of the current holding-tendency: t = 10 is covered by the exhaustive enumeration of Proposition 21-16(1), and t = 11–16 by the pointwise recomputation of (2); there is in addition the cycle evidence of (4)(B).  
　Conclusion (conditional): if the three host correspondences Corr-E, Corr-D and Corr-Ω are accepted, and the holding-tendency semantic carry-over “the selected current capacity corresponds to ReplayPotential” is accepted, then Law\_{𝔐21,t}(L\*, I) = 1 holds on H1 for 10 ≤ t ≤ 16 (forward read-back).  
　Not claimed: (A) Law = 0 for t \< 10. The negative end must go through the necessary direction of *The General Form of Law*, carrying C0 ∧ C2 ∧ H1 ∧ H2 ∧ H3, and must exclude every other carrying organization R′ (the failure of some item on the selected R does not by itself exclude other carryings); this exclusion has not been given, so the end of macro-step 10 is read only as “the first structural positive end of the selected R”, not as “the first formal Law”. (B) Histories other than H1, and times t \> 16. The forward and negative directions are not merged into a statement under one criterion.  
8.6  Proxy Readings of the Law Group Form Qualification, and Counterexamples (for Comparison; for the Formal Reading See 8.5)  
　Besides the formal reading of 8.5, this paper computes three kinds of proxy reading on R = {u\_P, c\_P}, I = {B, S}, W = 8 macro-steps, for comparison and counterexamples (C7L):  
　　DynamicRole^a (all-position reading): on the beats of the window \[t − W, t), an effective difference has been certified on T = R by \[17\] Definition 6′ (existential polarity), with all positions of R as the set positions.  
　　DynamicRole^h (formed-position reading; dyn\_formed in dm21\_run.py): as above, but the set positions at beat k are only the “formed positions”: oriented depth \> ε, and difference along the orientation from the rest-reference history (the same initial state, rest beats only) \> ε. The reference history and the actual history coincide at beat 0, so witnesses on the preset initial state (the old groove u\_P = 1/4) are not admitted.  
　　Ω^d (gully-depth proxy): for each c ∈ I, the window contains at least 2 events of type c at whose read R\* holds; min(u\_P, c\_P) \> 0 before each macro-step in the window and at t (connection read by gully depth); the window is divided into two equal segments, each containing such an event of c.  
　　Ω^c (class-labeled path reading; dm21\_model.replay\_hist\_path): for each c ∈ I, the candidate events are the events in the window of type c along path P (footslope node → gully-mouth segment → channel reach → mouth of P) at whose read R\* holds; the re-traversal records are the candidate events whose preceding same-class event in the window is also a candidate (a single above-threshold event counts only as a candidate); there are at least 2 records; the actual conductance g\_P \> 0 at every macro-step boundary in the window and at t, and the actual throughflow Q\_P \> 0 at every event beat in the window; each half of the window contains a re-traversal record of c. “Consecutive” refers to the sequence of same-class events within the window, not to adjacent event beats; conductance is checked at macro-step boundaries and throughflow at event beats. This reading applies only to fixed topology; using it for variable paths requires separate verification of disconnection between events, same-path identity and succession.  
　Results (C7L):  
　　H1: Established 00001111111111111; DynamicRole^a and DynamicRole^h both 01111111111111111; Ω^d and Ω^c all 0; Dom\_8 00001111111111111.  
　　H1m = (B·R³)⁸(S·R³·B·R³)⁴: Established as for H1; DynamicRole^a and DynamicRole^h as for H1; Ω^d 00000000000001111, Ω^c 00000000000000011; Dom\_8 00001111111111111.  
　　H2: Established, Ω^d, Ω^c and Dom\_8 all 0; DynamicRole^a 01111111111111111, DynamicRole^h 00000000011111111.  
　Counterexample F21-3 (preset witness, C7Le): the positive witness of DynamicRole^a in macro-step 1 of H2 lies at beat 0 of the full history, with set position u\_P, and at that beat the actual history and the rest reference are exactly the same; within the first macro-step the differences of u\_P from the rest reference at successive beats are −49/256, −23/128, −43/256 (mouth burial by low flow makes the old groove shallower than rest alone would). So this positive shows only that the proved process depends structurally on the preset old-groove depth (structural sensitivity); it does not issue signal formation and retention before beat 0. DynamicRole^h is 0 in macro-steps 1–8 of H2 and 1 from macro-step 9 on; its first witness is at beat 33 (rest-diffusion process, target c\_P, formed positions {c\_P}; c\_P was written by the high flow at beat 32 and is deeper than in the rest reference). This witness gives no evidence for “the two routes share equally”.  
　Counterexample F21-4 (gully-depth proxy, C7Lg, C7Lh): when both segments have depth 1 but P is truly disconnected (g\_P = 0), the connection sub-item of Ω^d still reads “connection maintained”; reading by actual conductance and actual throughflow at event beats correctly reads the disconnection. Without a declared disconnection, g\_P ≥ 1/8 \> 0 on u\_P, c\_P ∈ \[lo, hi\], so “the route edge exists” is always true, and even within this domain the gully-depth proxy is not equivalent to connection (it is a stronger depth condition). The gully-depth proxy is therefore not used to read connection.  
　Candidates and records (C7Li): at the end of macro-step 13 of H1m (window \[5, 13)), the candidate events along path P are high flows 5, 6, 7, 9, 11 and low flows 8, 10, 12, and the re-traversal records are high flows 6, 7, 9, 11 and low flows 10, 12; the low-flow records do not cover the first half of the window, so Ω^c = 0; it first equals 1 at the end of macro-step 15.  
　\[Conditional conclusion\] (1) Forward-looking dominance and historical support are separate: in H1, Dom\_8 is 1 from macro-step 4, while Ω^d and Ω^c are never 1 (the low flows of H1 all lie in the later part, so the class-labeled coverage is not satisfied). (2) In H1m, Established ∧ DynamicRole^h ∧ Ω^c first holds jointly at the end of macro-step 15 (C7Li; DynamicRole^a ∧ Ω^d at macro-step 13, C7Lb), at which point Dom\_8 = 1. (3) In the histories tested, wherever the three proxy items are 1 together, Dom\_8 is 1 (C7Lc, C7Li)—a proxy reading consistent in direction with SC\_Hold, which does not prove SC\_Hold. None of the proxy readings above is used for the formal items of 8.5.

9\. Intervention and Bearing  
9.1  Interventions in the Local Model: Capacity Reduction and Mouth Burial, True Disconnection, Sediment-Destination Switches

### 9.1.1  Protocol \[declared\]

　Object: the frame state w8. Interventions are applied to w8, followed by clipping and gridding; the criterion is the same Dom\_8(P8); in addition, the immediate main relation after the intervention (B, S) is read, as is Dom\_8 after the next 4 high flows. Interventions fall into three classes with separate semantics: (1) erasure and capacity reduction of Trace-Responses (changing Structural Position values); (2) disconnection (the declared disconnection switch: the conductance of P is set to 0, simulating closure of the gully mouth; verifiable: under disconnection Q\_P = 0); (3) process switches of sediment destination (acting only at the lake mouth of route P): periodic removal of the mouth bar (m\_P := 0 at the end of every macro-step, with the removed material exported), sustained prohibition of mouth-bar deposition with all channel-exit sediment going into the lake basin (β\_P = 0), and sustained prohibition of mouth-bar deposition with the share that would have stayed on the bar exported (β\_P = 0, export).  
　Two future protocols for periodic removal: (A) “formative history under periodic removal, then the P8 challenge after treatment stops”: in the history the bar is cleared at the end of every macro-step up to the read point, and the challenge uses ordinary macro-steps; (B) “continued-treatment challenge”: every macro-step of the challenge follows the frozen order “event beat → r rest beats → bar clearing m\_P := 0 → at most one perturbation” (dm21\_model.macro\_cleared). The two sets of decisions are output separately. In addition, the four cases are rerun with a relation rel\_basin(θ) whose target quantity is the sediment actually entering the lake basin (event throughflow of route P ≥ θQ, and P's sediment into the lake basin \> 0 and ≥ θ of the sum over routes of sediment into the lake basin) (the periodic-removal case uses continued-treatment macro-steps).

### 9.1.2  Results \[verified\] (C8)

　Erasing the gully-mouth incision, u\_P := 0: fails immediately; Dom\_8 = 0; after 4 high flows, Dom\_8 = 1.  
　Erasing the channel-reach incision, c\_P := 0: fails immediately; Dom\_8 = 0; after 4 high flows, Dom\_8 = 1.  
　Erasing the mouth bar, m\_P := 0; erasing the footslope deposit of the opposite route, u\_Q := 0; half-filling the channel reach, c\_P := c\_P/2: holds immediately; Dom\_8 = 1.  
　Capacity reduction by mouth burial, u\_P := lo: the conductance of P is still 455625/1888036, and both high and low flows have positive flow; fails immediately; Dom\_8 = 0; still 0 after 4 high flows (C8b). This is a capacity-reduction/mouth-burial intervention, not a severing of the water-passing connection.  
　True disconnection (C8c): under disconnection Q\_P = S\_P = 0, and Q bears all the inflow; Dom\_8 = 0. When the disconnection is removed after 4 high flows under disconnection, P does not regain dominance (Dom\_8 = 0), and Q's main relation does not hold for both inflow classes simultaneously either; when it is removed after 8 high flows under disconnection, Q carries the main throughflow and sediment transport for high flow but not for low flow (m\_Q = 177/256; the mouth-bar difference is tightest for low flow, Corollary 21-2a). \[Conditional conclusion\] Disconnection induces a transitional avulsion state: the original candidate dominance is lost, the new route has not yet acquired candidate dominance, and its hysteresis comes from the negative write-back of the new route's own mouth bar.  
　Sediment-destination switches (moderate backwater Lb = 1, the same H1; C8d, C8e, C8f; values are cumulative over the whole run, to four decimal places). The decision strings are, in order: challenge after treatment stops (ordinary macro-steps); continued-treatment challenge (periodic removal only); lake-entry target.  
　　No intervention: all 0; —; all 0. Mass accounts: channel-exit sediment of route P 6.7200, retained on the mouth bar 3.3600, into the lake basin 3.3600.  
　　Periodic removal of the mouth bar: 00011111111111111; 00011111111111111; 00011111111111111. Mass accounts: channel-exit sediment 18.5501, retained on the mouth bar at event beats 9.2750 (part of it later reworked away by lake waves), into the lake basin 9.2750, exported by periodic removal 3.8555.  
　　Sustained prohibition of mouth-bar deposition, all into the lake basin: 00011111111111111; —; 00011111111111111. Mass accounts: channel-exit sediment 18.5501, retained on the bar 0, into the lake basin 18.5501.  
　　Sustained prohibition of mouth-bar deposition, the share that would stay on the bar exported: 00011111111111111; —; 00011111111111111. Mass accounts: channel-exit sediment 18.5501, retained on the bar 0, into the lake basin 9.2750, exported 9.2750.  
　The successors under the two protocols differ (C8e, Counterexample F21-6): at the end of macro-step 3 of the periodic-removal history, the ordinary-macro-step successor at the next high flow has m\_P = 39/128, and the continued-treatment successor has m\_P = 0; yet in this computed example the two decision strings are identical position by position, both first reaching 1 at the end of macro-step 3. That the two strings coincide is a result of this example, not an equivalence of the two protocols.  
　Lake-entry target (C8f): in this example the sediment entering the lake basin via route Q is 0 in every case, and P's lake-entry share and channel-exit share are both 1, so the lake-entry target string coincides with the channel-exit target string; this coincidence is limited to this example: the β\_P variants make channel-exit sediment and lake-entry sediment no longer split in the same proportion, and the general case must be computed separately.  
　\[Conditional conclusion\] (1) All three classes of switch make the candidate dominance, which is otherwise not generated under moderate backwater, generate at the end of macro-step 3; their equivalence is limited to the declared throughflow and channel-exit readings (and, in this example, the lake-entry target reading): in 𝔐21 dominance depends only on whether the mouth bar retains sediment (V is never read). (2) Reallocation (sediment redirected into the lake basin) and export (sediment leaving the system) can have the same subsequent dynamics while their direct material outputs differ: into the lake basin 18.5501 vs. 9.2750, exported 0 vs. 9.2750; the two are reported separately. (3) The absolute amounts of sediment received by the lake basin differ; in the extended model 𝔐21⁺, where the lake basin can feed back, this changes what follows via lake level and backwater (Section 11). Physical dredging must distinguish these three classes of operation.

### 9.1.3  Interpretation

　(1) Key Trace-Responses: the gully-mouth incision and the channel-reach incision of P (consistent with Theorem 21-2); erasing either one loses dominance, which can be rebuilt by high flows while the other Trace-Responses remain (hysteresis). (2) The mouth bar, the footslope deposit of the opposite route and a half-filled channel reach are not key. (3) The water-passing connection is tested by true disconnection: disconnection loses dominance; capacity reduction by mouth burial loses dominance and does not recover within 4 high flows, but it is not disconnection. (4) The bearer of the negative write-back is the part “retained on the mouth bar” of the sediment-transport connection from channel reach to mouth bar.  
9.2  Before and After Formation, Interventions and Failure \[verified\] (dm21\_run\_law)  
　Scope: the frozen model, candidate relation and bearers of Theorem 21-17 are kept; this section additionally lists comparisons such as H1, H1m and H★, each with its own time window (H★ has 25 read points, the ends of macro-steps 0–24). The range of macro-steps 10–16 of H1 in Theorem 21-17 governs only the conditional read-back of Law.  
(1) Before and after formation under the same criterion (L1, L2, L4): at the ends of macro-steps 0–3 of H1, Established = 0, and what is missing is the “read-out holds” part of CarrierRealizes; Established first equals 1 at the end of macro-step 4 (the same time as the first Dom\_8); at the ends of macro-steps 4–9, ΩSupport = 0, and at the end of macro-step 9 what is missing is the third segment-level fact (the first half of the window \[1, 9) contains only the first bearing pass, event 4, and no re-traversal record); at the end of macro-step 10 all three segment-level facts hold. H1m and H★ receive the same decision at the end of macro-step 10, but their earlier parts differ (the 9th and 10th events are SS in H1 and SB in H1m and H★). H2 has Established = 0 throughout. Distinction of criteria: the segment-level result at macro-step 10 is not labeled by inflow class and does not imply that high and low flows have each re-traversed in both halves of the window; the class-labeled strict reading is 0 throughout H1 and first equals 1 in H1m at macro-step 15 (L4d). Entry coverage holds separately for B and S: at the end of macro-step 10 the conductances of the two series segments are positive, and for both inflow classes the actual Q\_P is positive and the duty reading holds (L4c).  
(2) The bearer is the historical increment (L3): at the end of macro-step 10 of H1, under both inflow classes, “reverting u\_P and c\_P to the contemporaneous rest reference (keeping the preset base)” changes all of Q\_P, S\_P, Δu\_P, Δc\_P and flips the read-out 1 → 0; the difference in the activity-mediated A branch of the channel-reach write-back scour\_c@c\_P is about −0.434754 (high flow) and −0.071095 (low flow), and the difference in the direct-read D branch is 0 in both cases; after clipping and gridding, with each arm first subtracting its own pre-beat value, the difference in single-beat write increment (actual arm minus reversion arm) is 7/16 and 9/128, which rules out mistaking the pre-existing incision difference for a write difference newly added at this beat (L3e). In parallel, at macro-step 15 of H1m: 109/256 and 7/128. Macro-step by macro-step, “reverting the both-position increments flips the read-out” holds from macro-step 4 on. At the ends of macro-steps 4–6 of H2, reverting the increments turns the read-out from failing to holding: what prevents the relation is the negative mouth-burial increment written by history. DynamicRole is 1 throughout (at macro-step 0 it is borne by the preset old groove), so it does not by itself distinguish before and after formation.  
(3) Interventions (L6, L8; history H★ = (B·R³)⁸(S·R³·B·R³)⁸; the structural positive end of the same R first holds at the end of macro-step 10). Naming: we speak only of “first occurrence, exit or regaining of the structural positive end of the same R”, read in three columns: water-route relation (Established), re-traversal window (ΩSupport) and current capacity (Cap\_8). Trace-Response interventions are applied at the end of macro-step k (before the (k + 1)th event); “disconnection @ k” means that P's conductance is set to 0 for the (k + 1)th event, the one after the end of macro-step k (program t == k).  
　Reverting the both-position increments (keeping the preset base): when they are reverted at the end of macro-step 2, 4, 6 or 8, the structural positive end does not appear within 25 macro-steps; when they are reverted at the end of macro-step 12 or 16, the structural positive end exits immediately and is not regained within 25 macro-steps; in these cases the water-route relation is lost at the ends of macro-steps 12–19, after which the re-traversal window is missing for lack of records (L6a, L6e).  
　Single-position reversion: reverting c\_P at the end of macro-step 2, the structural positive end does not appear within 25 macro-steps; reverting u\_P delays it to macro-step 19; reverting either position at the end of macro-step 12 or 16, it exits and is regained two macro-steps later (L6b).  
　Erasing the mouth bar: the mouth bar is not part of the R selected here; in the interventions tested, its erasure did not prevent the structural positive end (erasure at the end of macro-step 2 brings the first occurrence forward to macro-step 9; at the ends of macro-steps 4, 6, 8 nothing changes; at the ends of macro-steps 12, 16 it does not exit); the mouth bar is still the bearer of the negative write-back (5.6.2) (L6c).  
　Disconnection for one event: disconnecting the 3rd or 5th event (k = 2, 4), the structural positive end does not appear within 25 macro-steps; for the 7th or 9th event (k = 6, 8), the first occurrence is delayed to macro-step 17; for the 13th event (k = 12), the structural positive end exits from the end of macro-step 12 (that read already includes the zero-conductance boundary), the water-route relation is not lost and only the re-traversal window is missing (Brk\_W = 1), and it is regained at the end of macro-step 21 (when the events and boundaries of the window \[13, 21) no longer include that event); for the 17th event (k = 16), it is not regained within 25 macro-steps (L6d, L6e).  
　Current-capacity column: wherever the structural positive end holds on the above 30 trajectories from the intervention time on, deduplication by the projection removing V gives 207 states, and Cap\_8 is 1 at all of them (L6f; default successor protocol, no sustained intervention; the semantic carry-over is a separate condition).  
　Physical-reading column (L8; reads directly the Q\_P/Q of both inflow classes and the complete duty Read\_B ∧ Read\_S, without substituting Established for throughflow): with both-position reversion at the end of macro-step 12, the main throughflow of both classes and the complete duty are 0 at the ends of macro-steps 12–19, recover at the end of macro-step 20 and are maintained to the end of macro-step 24, while the structural positive end has not been regained by the end of macro-step 24 (L8a); with both-position reversion at the end of macro-step 16, the main throughflow of both classes and the duty recover at the end of macro-step 24, and the structural positive end is not regained (L8b); with only c\_P reverted at the end of macro-step 12, the main throughflow of both classes recovers at the end of macro-step 13 and the structural positive end is regained at the end of macro-step 14 (L8c); with both-position reversions before the first formation (at every integer read point among the ends of macro-steps 2–8), the main throughflow of both classes is not attained from the intervention to the end of macro-step 24 (L8d); with disconnection of the 13th or 17th event, P's throughflow is zero at the actually disconnected event itself, the capacities for both classes, read with normal conductance at each macro-step end, hold, and at the next inflow after the gate is lifted (a high flow) the duty reading holds (L8e). Physical recovery and recovery of re-traversal qualification occur at two different times; “not regained” for the structural positive end is not read as “physical throughflow has not recovered”.  
9.3  Derivation of the Failure Conditions \[proved\] \[verified\] (L7)  
　By Theorem 21-2(1), the low-flow item of Established requires m\_P − m\_Q \< (1 − θ)Q\_S/g\_Q; this holds at every structural positive end in the three histories (L7a); one grid step past this bound, the low-flow read-out fails and Established = 0 (L7b). By the second fact of Corr-Ω, a disconnection within the window makes ΩSupport = 0 until the disconnection leaves the window; by the third, ΩSupport is lost as soon as either half of the window has no re-traversal record. Breachable predictions are listed separately in Section 13 (P21-11–P21-13).

10\. How Climate Laws Remain in Landforms: Distinguishable and Indistinguishable

### 10.1  Protocol \[declared\]

　Family of seasonal orders: four seasonal blocks per year (freeze-thaw block F⁴, high-flow block B·R³, two low-flow blocks S·R³), in 12 permutations, each repeated for 4 years; cumulative precipitation, strippable upper limit, number of freeze-thaw beats and number of rest beats are identical item by item, and the initial terrain is the same; two hillslope cases: ample sediment supply (h₀ = 3) and limited sediment supply (h₀ = 0). The reading set consists of topography, hillslope regolith, lake-basin cumulative amount, annual layers and event layers; the “lake-basin annual layer” is obtained by summing event layers by year with known year boundaries. Real annual varves require separate, independent dating and genetic criteria (the seasonal origin and year-by-year dating of varves described in the abstract of Zolitschka et al. 2015), and the annual layers of this model do not replace these criteria.  
10.2  Theorem 21-9 (Climate Trace-Leaving)  
　(A) Exchange indistinguishability \[proved\]  Antecedents (they must hold for each history compared, one by one): (1) h ≥ s\_max(E) at every event arrival (supply saturation); (2) h never hits the upper limit hmax throughout (no capping); (3) ft, wd and s\_max are all integer multiples of 1/G (grid-point antecedent, making additions to and subtractions from h exact under gridding). Conclusion: two histories that differ only in the positions of freeze-thaw beats give final states identical position by position and event layers identical event by event. Proof: by (1), σ = s\_max(E) does not depend on h, and the action of an event on u, c, m, V does not read h; the action of an event on h is the subtraction of s\_max, which is exact under gridding by (3) and does not hit the clipping bounds by (2); F only adds ft to h, and R adds wd to h and acts position-wise on the other positions without reading h (C9e: F and R commute position by position, also after gridding). Hence F commutes with the map of any beat: on h it is addition or subtraction of grid multiples, and on the other positions F is the identity. Swapping freeze-thaw beats one at a time with adjacent beats turns one freeze-thaw order into another; each swap changes neither the final state nor the u, c, m, V components of the pre-beat state of any event beat, and so does not change the event layers. The antecedents must hold on every intermediate history of the swap chain; we prove this with the upper and lower envelopes of the beat-by-beat cumulative freeze-thaw counts (without requiring the freeze-thaw beats to move in one direction). The two end histories X, Y have the same sequence of non-freeze-thaw beats; let n\_X(i), n\_Y(i) be the number of freeze-thaw beats before the ith non-freeze-thaw beat (nondecreasing in i, with the same final value). Under (1)(2)(3), h only undergoes additions and subtractions of grid multiples without hitting the clipping bounds, so for any history Z of the same kind the value of h before the ith non-freeze-thaw beat is h\_base(i) + ft·n\_Z(i), where h\_base(i) is determined by the non-freeze-thaw beats alone. Take n\_M(i) = min(n\_X(i), n\_Y(i)) (still nondecreasing with the same final value; it defines a legitimate history M). From X to M, freeze-thaw beats are only moved later; each adjacent swap decreases some n(i) by 1 and keeps n\_M ≤ n ≤ n\_X; from M to Y, freeze-thaw beats are only moved earlier, keeping n\_M ≤ n ≤ n\_Y. So the counts of every intermediate history on the chain lie everywhere within the envelope \[min(n\_X, n\_Y), max(n\_X, n\_Y)\], and its h values lie within the upper and lower envelopes of the corresponding values of the two end histories (likewise for beats lying among the freeze-thaw beats, counted by the cumulative number before the beat). Antecedent (1) is a lower-bound condition on h and (2) an upper-bound condition; holding at both ends, they hold for all values within the envelope. ∎ \[verified\] The antecedents hold on each of the 12 histories in the ample-supply case (the minimum h at events is 81/64 ≥ s\_max; h ≤ 7/2 \< hmax throughout; C0b checks the grid-point antecedent); the number of exact classes is 3 (C9a).  
　(B) Limited sediment supply \[verified\] (C9b): numbers of exact classes: topography 12, lake-basin event layers 11 (FSSB and SSBF are indistinguishable; for the reason see 10.3), annual layers 9, cumulative amount 9, hillslope 4, joint 12.  
　(C) Observational compression \[proved\]: annual layers are a function of event layers, and the cumulative amount is a function of annual layers; when a reading Y′ is a function of Y, equivalence classes only merge, and the number of classes does not increase (C9c). This clause deals only with compression by observation functions, not with measurement resolution.  
　(D) Finite resolution (separate from (C)) \[verified\]: define the separation margin d(Y) of a reading Y as the minimum, over pairs of distinct classes, of the maximum componentwise difference (C9 output). Limited supply: topography 5/256, hillslope 1/8, lake-basin cumulative amount 3/256, annual layers 1/64, event layers 1/64, joint 25/256; ample supply: topography 15/128, event layers 5/8, joint 15/128. If the reading error is bounded by e, then for e \< d(Y)/2 the exact classes remain pairwise distinguishable in the field; for e ≥ d(Y)/2 pairwise distinguishability of all candidates cannot be guaranteed, and overlap must be judged pair by pair (for some pairs of classes the componentwise difference may still exceed 2e). Example: with limited supply, distinguishing all 12 orders by topography requires e \< 5/512 ≈ 0.0098 (C9d). The number of exact classes is a model conclusion, while distinguishability in the field depends on the error budget; the two are accounted separately.  
　(E) Precipitation order (entry comparison, C9g): H1 and H2 are distinguished by the topography reading (in one, P acquires candidate dominance; in the other, the two routes are symmetric position by position in the final state, Theorem 21-7(B)), and their lake-basin cumulative amounts differ.  
　(F) Finite-window readings under a common subsequent drive (C9f): under the common subsequent drive (B·R³·S·R³·S·R³)¹⁰, all 30 reads of the main relation from the final state of H1 hold, while from the final state of H2 the two routes remain symmetric position by position throughout; the two final states do not converge within this window. This is a finite-window reading; it does not test competing theory E, which declares only long-term convergence (12.8, Counterexample F21-7), and it is not extrapolated to an infinite horizon.  
10.3  What Trace-Responses the Climate Laws Leave, and Their Organization  
　\[Conditional conclusion\] (1) The seasonal order of high and low flows remains in the organization of footslope, channel reach and lake mouth (8.3). (2) The seasonal order of freeze-thaw and inflow leaves traces only on supply-limited hillslopes, its main carriers being the regolith deficit on the hillslope, the footslope deposit and the lake-basin event layers; with ample supply it leaves no trace at all. (3) The lake-basin event layers cannot distinguish FSSB from SSBF: in both orders the freeze-thaw regolith is stripped by the two low flows before the high flow, while low flows in the divided-flow state drop all incoming sediment at the footslope and deliver 0 to the lake; by the time of the high flow the hillslope regolith in both is down to 3/64, and the lake entry of the high flows is identical event by event (the C9 output lists the events one by one); the difference remains only in the footslope deposit. The lake-basin record “sees” only the part of the climatic signal that reaches the lake basin.  
　Relation to signal shredding (Jerolmack and Paola 2010): this paper gives two kinds of exact, position-by-position non-retention of traces (map commutation, mouth-burial erasure), which lie at a different level from the statistical signal attenuation of that paper; that paper supports only the filtering proposition it states and does not endorse the readings of this paper.

11\. River–Lake Feedback and Avulsion Succession: the Extended Model 𝔐21⁺

### 11.1  Purpose and Scope

　The domain of the paper includes the lake shore and lake basin and their linkage with the channels, streams and gullies. In 𝔐21 the lake basin V is recorded but never read, the routes are fixed at two, and disconnection is an external intervention; the model contains neither the feedback “the lake-basin state changes another waterway” nor the process by which, after an endogenous avulsion, the duty is taken over by a new route. This section joins the two within one extended model 𝔐21⁺: the storage-release and deposition state of the lake basin actually enters the reading and writing of at least two waterways; paths can open, close and reorganize through scour and deposition (a crevasse creates a new channel, an abandoned route heals). The duty relation and criteria are frozen in advance, and four categories are reported separately along the actual trajectory. 𝔐21 is kept as the local baseline: it is the restriction of 𝔐21⁺ to the shared positions under “lake level clamped at ℓ₀ = 0, crevassing disabled” (Proposition 21-11). The time window is still an interval in which new tectonic effects are negligible; there is as yet no measured certification on real landforms (U21-12).  
11.2  Structural Positions and New Mechanism Laws of 𝔐21⁺ (Source and Status Item by Item; code/dm21\_lake.py)  
　Structural Positions (17): u\_P, c\_P, m\_P, u\_Q, c\_Q, m\_Q, h, V of 𝔐21, plus u\_N, c\_N, m\_N of the latent new channel N and the existence variable e\_N ∈ {0, 1} (the existence variable of \[18\] D18-12), the maximum incision depths ever reached by the channel reaches of the routes, x\_P, x\_Q, x\_N, the lake water volume WL, and the incision c\_D of the lake outflow channel. New parameters (dimensionless constructed values): AL = 8, kV = 1/32, ko = 1/2, z0 = 1/2, SD = 3/4, keD = 1/4, ωcD = 1/8, ev = 1/16, Hs = 3/10, d0 = 1/4, lenN = 1/2, S0N = 3/2; clipping WL ∈ \[0, 64\], c\_D ∈ \[0, 1/2\], x\_j ∈ \[0, 3\]; everything else as in 𝔐21.  
　L9  Lake level \[Representational choice\]: ℓ = WL/AL + kV·V. Lake water volume raises the water level, and so does shallowing of the lake basin by deposition (base-level rise). Clamp arm (clamp): ℓ ≡ ℓ₀ (WL and V are accounted as usual but enter no read-out).  
　L10  Lake level enters backwater \[Published mechanism: backwater-zone aggradation and base-level change alter avulsion (Ganti 2016, main text: at low flow the upstream part of the backwater zone aggrades, and 78% of avulsions occur during high-flow events; Chadwick 2022, abstract: avulsions become more frequent when sea-level rise accelerates); here a direct generalization of L5\]: for every existing route j, the channel-reach slope is S\_c(j) = (S0\_j − (m\_j + ℓ)/Lb)⁺, with ℓ given by L9 (in 𝔐21, ℓ is the constant 0 declared with the event). So one and the same lake level enters the scour-and-deposition writes of P and Q (and N) simultaneously.  
　L11  Lake outflow channel D \[Representational choice: linearization of weir flow\]: potential outflow QD\_pot = ko·(ℓ − (z0 − c\_D))⁺ (spillway-sill height z0 − c\_D); realized outflow QD = min(QD\_pot, available water), where the available water is WL + Q at event beats (inflow of the same beat) and WL at rest beats, and there is no outflow at freeze-thaw beats (clipping does not count the requested amount under water shortage as actual throughflow); at every beat WL decreases by QD; at rest beats WL also decreases by evaporation min(ev·WL, WL − QD); at event beats WL increases by the inflow Q. The incision of D follows the above-threshold scour of the realized outflow, Δc\_D = keD·(QD·SD − ωcD)⁺ (the same form as L3), and at rest beats c\_D backfills at rate κ. When there is enough surplus water (QD ≤ (1 − ev)·WL), evaporation is simply ev·WL; under water shortage the realized outflow is capped by the available water (Counterexample F21-5, X1c). D is the third waterway that reads the lake-basin state (it reads ℓ and writes c\_D and WL).  
　L12  Channel-depth memory \[Representational choice\]: at event beats x\_j := max(x\_j, c\_j + Δc\_j) (the maximum incision depth ever reached by the channel reach); at rest beats x\_j backfills at the same rate as c\_j (κ). The in-channel aggradation is x\_j − c\_j, and the channel depth is h0 + x\_j.  
　L13  Crevasse (superelevation criterion) \[Published form: Ganti 2016, main text, reports an experimental avulsion threshold of about 0.3hc; Chadwick 2022, abstract, describes repeated avulsion when the river aggrades to nearly half the channel depth; Slingerland and Smith 2004, main text, describes superelevation normalized by channel depth, the water-surface-slope advantage of the crevasse channel over the parent channel as the core of avulsion, and triggering by events (floods) that abruptly change conveyance capacity\]: before the event beat of a high flow B, if e\_N = 0 and some existing parent route j ∈ {P, Q} satisfies x\_j − c\_j ≥ Hs·(h0 + x\_j), then N is created at that beat: e\_N := 1, u\_N = c\_N = x\_N := d0. Hs = 3/10 takes the form of the value reported by Ganti 2016 (declared, not calibrated); triggering occurs only at high flow, corresponding to “high-flow triggering”.  
　L14  The new channel is a shorter path \[Representational choice; based on the slope advantage in Slingerland and Smith 2004, main text\]: the conductance of N is divided by the length factor lenN = 1/2 (short path), and its channel-reach slope is S0N = 3/2 (\> S0 = 3/4). N is connected in parallel with P and Q at the same footslope node; Kirchhoff flow division among the three routes (successively removing routes whose sill height is not below the node head; no backflow). When e\_N = 0, N carries no read-out and no write.  
　L15  Healing of abandoned routes \[Published mechanism: the healing of abandoned channels and its attraction–repulsion effect on later avulsions, as described in the abstract of Martin and Edmonds 2022; only the simplest form is taken here\]: no separate healing law is set up; the gully mouth, channel reach and channel-depth memory of an abandoned route are backfilled by the rest diffusion of L8 (κ). So the model contains only one kind of healing, “recovery toward the plain surface”, and no spatial differentiation of healing rates.  
　L16  Sediment-destination switch of route P: betaP and exportP use the same accounting split as path\_terms in 𝔐21 (4.2 L6): the mouth-bar retention rate of route P is β\_P, and the balance not retained on the bar is reallocated into the lake basin or exported from the system; the exported amount is accounted separately and enters no position.  
　Post-processing as in 𝔐21 (clipping and gridding, G = 256, by jhc\_core.Host.step). All values are Fraction, with no randomness (7.1 applies).  
11.3  Embedding and Lake-Basin Read-Out \[verified\] \[proved\]  
　Proposition 21-11 (Embedding of the local baseline) \[proved\]  Working domain: the event class of 𝔐21 (event lake level ℓ = 0); 𝔐21⁺ with clamp = True, ℓ₀ = 0, crevassing disabled; the two models take the same parameters and the same switch semantics (closed, betaP, exportP); the initial state of 𝔐21⁺ equals that of 𝔐21 on the 8 positions of 𝔐21, with e\_N = 0. Conclusion: under any beat order, 𝔐21⁺ coincides with 𝔐21 on the 8 positions of 𝔐21, beat by beat.  
　Proof: by induction on the number of beats; suppose the two pre-beat states coincide on the shared positions. (1) e\_N is written only by crevassing, so with crevassing disabled it is always 0; hence N does not exist and enters neither flow division nor writes. (2) Equal flow division: 𝔐21 uses the truncated closed form of L2, 𝔐21⁺ uses successive removal. If g\_P, g\_Q \> 0: raw ≥ 0 ⇔ Q + g\_Q(m\_Q − m\_P) ≥ 0 ⇔ φ ≥ m\_P, and raw ≤ Q ⇔ φ ≥ m\_Q (φ = (Q + g\_Pm\_P + g\_Qm\_Q)/(g\_P + g\_Q)); when φ \> m\_P, m\_Q, both give Q\_j = g\_j(φ − m\_j); when φ ≤ m\_Q, the closed form truncates to Q\_P = Q, and after removing Q the single route gives Q\_P = Q; φ ≤ m\_P is symmetric; the two cannot hold at once, because Σg\_j(φ − m\_j) = Q \> 0. If some g\_j = 0 (disconnection), both give all the inflow to the other route (both give 0 if the other route is also at 0). (3) Equal components: both models call the same pterms for P and Q, and the lake-level argument ℓ₀ = 0 equals the event lake level 0; the sediment supply σ has the same formula; the accounting split of betaP and exportP in 𝔐21⁺ is formula by formula the same as in path\_terms of 𝔐21. (4) Equal writes: the component lists for the shared positions at rest, freeze-thaw and event beats are identical item by item in the two models (the extra components of 𝔐21⁺ write only non-shared positions such as x\_j, WL, c\_D, e\_N); jhc\_core.Host.step sums by position and then clips and grids position by position, and the clipping bounds of the shared positions are the same. Hence the shared positions coincide after the beat. ∎ \[verified\] X0a (40 beats) and X0b (5 parameter-and-switch settings × 16 sequences, 80 histories in all) are checks within this domain. Outside the domain (nonzero event lake level, clamp off, crevassing allowed) no embedding is claimed.  
　Lemma 21-12 (Two-route reading and writing of the lake-basin state) \[proved\]  (1) ℓ is strictly increasing in WL and in V (AL, kV \> 0). (2) For every existing route j, S\_c(j) is nonincreasing in ℓ, and strictly decreasing where S\_c(j) \> 0; the channel-reach transport capacity and scour rate kt(Q\_jS\_c − ωt)⁺, ke(Q\_jS\_c − ωc)⁺ are nonincreasing in ℓ, and their strict decrease further requires Q\_j \> 0 and a positive argument of the corresponding positive part (Q\_jS\_c \> ωt or \> ωc); under these conditions a rise in ℓ reduces, at the same beat, the channel-reach capacity or scour of P and Q (and N) simultaneously (read), and via L4 changes their channel-reach scour-and-deposition writes (write). (3) The potential outflow is nondecreasing in ℓ and in c\_D; its strict increase requires ko \> 0 and ℓ \> z0 − c\_D; the realized outflow is in addition capped by the available water (L11), and when the cap is active it does not increase with ℓ. (4) Under the clamp arm, ℓ in (1)–(3) is a constant, and changes in WL and V enter no read-out. Proof: directly from the rational expressions of L9–L11. ∎ \[verified\] X1a: on the initial terrain ι with a high flow, WL := 8 or V := 32 both raise ℓ from 0 to 1; the channel-reach backwater slopes of both P and Q fall from 3/4 to 1/2; the channel-reach scour and deposition change from 0.3242, 0.1758 to −0.1319, −0.1442 (both routes switch from scour to deposition); the potential outflow at the lake outlet changes from 0 to 1/4, and the realized outflow at the high-flow beat is 1/4; with V := 32 and WL = 0, the available water at rest beats is 0, the realized outflow is 0, the final water volume is 0 and c\_D is unchanged (X1c, outlet boundary); X1b: under the clamp arm the same changes alter neither the scour and deposition of the two routes nor the potential outflow.  
11.4  Protocol, Duty Relation and Criteria Frozen in Advance \[declared\]  
　Common starting point w₀: run the formative history HF8 = (B·R³)⁸ in the clamp arm (that is, the frame state w8 of 𝔐21), then set WL = V = 0 (lake empty, lake-basin deposits cleared) and e\_N = 0. w₀ has u\_P ≈ 2.168, c\_P ≈ 1.637, m\_P ≈ 0.504, u\_Q ≈ −0.059, c\_Q ≈ 0.051, x\_P ≈ 1.637, h = 19/8 (X0 output).  
　Fixed inflow: 15 years, each with “4 freeze-thaw beats, 1 high flow, 2 low flows” (3 rest beats after each event), i.e., (F⁴·B·R³·S·R³·S·R³)¹⁵, identical for both arms and for all variants. The only switch compared is lake-basin feedback (clamp), crevassing (avulse) or the lake-outlet parameters (ko, z0).  
　Duty ρ\* (frozen): route j carries the main throughflow (Q\_j ≥ θQ, θ = 2/3) for both inflow classes B and S and carries the main sediment flux, with sediment flux taken as channel-exit sediment out (channel-exit sediment \> 0 and ≥ θ of the sum of channel-exit sediment over existing routes; the default metric = out of role\_holder in the program). This is the same reading of R\*\_θ of 4.3 applied to multiple routes. By default all routes have the same bar-retention rate (β \< 1), so whenever there is sediment the channel-exit share equals the actual lake-entry share, and the original witness is kept (the lowest actual lake-entry share of N during the transition is 0.927692); when an intervention changes the bar-retention rate of one route, the two are read separately (X5d: the two registration strings of the eight arms are identical position by position, while the mass accounts differ).  
　Same-kind relation (weaker, frozen): route j takes the majority of throughflow (Q\_j \> Q/2) on both B and S.  
　Initial holders holders₀ = the set of holders of ρ\* on w₀ = {P} (X2 output).  
　Registration (read before every event arrival; four mutually exclusive categories): “original path kept”: some route in holders₀ still bears ρ*; “new path bears same duty”: no route in holders₀ bears it, and another route bears ρ*; “same-kind relation only”: no route bears ρ\*, but some route has the same-kind relation; “duty interrupted”: neither. Also registered: avulsion (parent route, event index, full-history beat index, criterion value, lake level), first bearing by the new route, and healing and failure of the abandoned route (the abandoned route's high-flow share \< θ).  
　These criteria were written into dm21\_lake.role\_holder, same\_kind and classify before running, and were not changed after running.  
11.5  Results: Feedback-On Arm and Clamp Arm \[verified\] (X2, X3)  
　Clamp arm (ℓ ≡ 0): no crevasse throughout; all 45 events are “original path kept” (X2b).  
　Feedback-on arm: the registration string is OOOOOOOOOOOOOkkxkkxkkkkkkNNNNNNNNNNNNNNNNNNNN (O original path kept, k same-kind relation only, x duty interrupted, N new path bears same duty).  
　　Formation: during the first 12 events the lake fills from empty, and via backwater the rising lake level causes in-channel aggradation in the channel reach of P (Lemma 21-12(2)).  
　　Avulsion: at the 13th event (beat 68 of the full history, a high flow), parent route P, with pre-beat x\_P − c\_P = 0.3633 ≥ Hs·(h0 + x\_P) = 0.3598 and lake level 0.6580; N is created (X3a). Before the avulsion, P kept the duty throughout.  
　　Transition: events 14–25 alternate between “same-kind relation only” (10 times, each time with N as the holder of the same-kind relation) and “duty interrupted” (2 times); what is missing is throughflow share: during the transition N's share of channel-exit sediment is at least 0.9277 for both inflow classes (the channel-exit sediment of P and Q is 0 or very small, P being in channel-reach aggradation), but N's throughflow share lies between 0.4647 and 0.6442, short of θ; the two registrations at which the share fell below 1/2 are duty interrupted (X3e).  
　　Succession: from the 26th event to the end of the window, all 20 events are “new path bears same duty”, with N as the holder (X3b).  
　　Counts of the four categories: original path kept 13, new path bears same duty 20, same-kind relation only 10, duty interrupted 2.  
　　Failure and healing: at the avulsion, P has u\_P = 1.3828, c\_P = −0.1641 (the channel reach has already aggraded above the initial surface); at the end of the window u\_P = −0.1602, c\_P = −0.0586, and P's high-flow division is 0.3110/4 \< θ (X3c): the gully-mouth incision of P has disappeared, the in-channel aggradation of its channel reach is recovering toward the plain surface, and P has failed.  
　Readings of the other waterway (X2a): the two arms have the same inflow (total event inflow 127.5). Cumulative event throughflow of route Q: 20.5988 in the feedback-on arm, 19.6021 in the clamp arm; Q's channel-reach scour and deposition is 0 in both arms (the scour threshold is not exceeded): Q is affected by lake-basin feedback only in throughflow, not in scour and deposition, and this is reported as it is. Lake outflow channel D: cumulative outflow 76.8867 in the feedback-on arm, with c\_D incised to the upper limit 1/2; outflow 0 and c\_D = 0 in the clamp arm. Cumulative throughflow of P: 46.3563 in the feedback-on arm, 107.8979 in the clamp arm; of N: 60.5449 in the feedback-on arm.  
　Crevassing-disabled arm (feedback on, X3d): registration string OOOOOOOOOOOOOOOkOOkOkkOkkOkkOkkOkkOkkOkkOkkOk; the first 13 are the same as in the feedback-on arm; thereafter P intermittently loses the duty (“same-kind relation only” recurs), and no bearing by a new path appears. \[Conditional conclusion\] In the trajectories tested, lake-basin feedback makes the original route lose stable bearing, and crevassing is the mechanism that bears the succession of the duty by a new route; without crevassing there is only intermittent maintenance by the original route and the same-kind relation.  
　The three times of “avulsion” are registered separately (X3f): opening of the new route, at the 13th event (a high flow); first majority of the actual inflow taken by the new route, at the 14th event (a low flow); first fulfillment of the complete duty by the new route, at the 26th event (a low flow). The three differ; from the opening occurring at a high flow one may not infer that the majority was also first taken at a high flow.  
11.6  Condition Table \[verified\] (X4; inflow as above, other parameters at their defaults)  
　ko = 1/4, z0 = 1/4: avulsion at event 13; the last 10 events all register as “new path bears same duty”; final lake level 1.2219.  
　ko = 1/4, z0 = 1/2: avulsion at event 10; final segment “new path bears same duty”; final lake level 1.2902.  
　ko = 1/2, z0 = 1/4: no avulsion; final segment “original path kept”; final lake level 0.5938.  
　ko = 1/2, z0 = 1/2 (default): avulsion at event 13; final segment “new path bears same duty”; final lake level 0.8712.  
　ko = 1, 2, z0 = 1/4, 1/2: no avulsion in any case; final segment “original path kept”; final lake level 0.42–0.48.  
　\[Conditional conclusion\] (1) The outcome is conditional: with high lake-outlet outflow capacity (ko ≥ 1) the original route is kept; with low outflow capacity and a high spillway sill the lake level rises, the original route aggrades, a crevasse opens, and a new route takes over (X4a). (2) Monotone readings on the tested grid: at fixed ko, a higher sill makes avulsion earlier or makes it appear where there was none; at fixed sill height, a lower outflow capacity makes avulsion earlier or makes it appear where there was none (X4b). (3) Every case whose final segment registers as “new path bears same duty” has a higher final lake level than every case whose final segment registers as “original path kept” (X4c). These are adjudicated only on 8 grid points and are not extrapolated into a boundary theorem on parameter space (U21-11).  
11.7  Comparison of the Three Sediment Destinations with Lake-Basin Feedback On \[verified\] (X5)  
　Protocol: same starting point w₀, same inflow (F⁴·B·R³·S·R³·S·R³)¹⁵, same default parameters, same crevasse switch; four arms: no intervention; periodic mouth-bar removal (m\_P := 0 after each event block, removed material exported and booked as removal); continuous suppression of mouth-bar deposition with the sand rerouted to the lake (β\_P = 0); continuous suppression of mouth-bar deposition with export (β\_P = 0, exported). Each arm is paired with a clamp arm (ℓ ≡ 0). The duty criterion is as in 11.4 (channel-exit sediment), with a second reading on the sediment actually entering the lake.  
　Feedback on: no intervention, avulsion at event 13, first majority of N at event 14, first bearing at event 26; periodic removal, events 16, 17 and 28; rerouting to the lake, events 16, 17 and 27; export, events 16, 17 and 28. Sediment budget (four decimals): channel-exit sediment of route P 8.0578, 10.9269, 10.1484, 10.4076; total into the lake basin 19.7844, 19.9292, 24.7294, 19.8652; exported 0, 0, 0, 5.2038; periodic removal 0, 2.4531, 0, 0; final lake level 0.8712, 0.8734, 0.9404, 0.8729.  
　Clamped: no crevasse in any of the four arms, and all 45 events register as “original path kept”; channel-exit sediment of route P 39.0628, 53.3463, 53.0650, 53.0650; into the lake basin 19.5314, 26.6732, 53.0650, 26.5325; exported 0, 0, 0, 26.5325; periodic removal 0, 11.2539, 0, 0.  
　\[Conditional conclusion\] (1) All three treatments delay the opening of the new channel (13 → 16). (2) With the lake level clamped, the four arms are indistinguishable on the specified readings of the tested protocol (opening, event-by-event duty register), while their material budgets differ; with lake-level feedback, the three treatments share the same opening time, but the lake level at the same end of observation and the succession time differ (rerouting to the lake gives the highest final lake level and the earliest succession). “The three treatments are equivalent” holds only without lake-level feedback, and only for the specified readings. (3) The two sediment-flux criteria, channel-exit and lake-entering, give the same duty-register string, while the material budgets differ. (4) These results are limited to formative history and duty registration; no perturbation challenge has been frozen on 𝔐21⁺ (neither the stop-treatment nor the continued-treatment protocol has run results on 𝔐21⁺), so future dominance under perturbation is not adjudicated by these results; the formal law formation of N and future dominance under perturbation on 𝔐21⁺ are separate questions.  
11.8  Interpretation: How River–Lake Feedback Generates, Interrupts and Carries Forward a Candidate Law of Geomorphic Organization  
　(1) The lake basin is no longer just a sink: lake-basin deposits V and lake water volume WL enter, via the lake level, the backwater and lake-outlet outflow of every existing route (Lemma 21-12). This is a closed loop of “activity acting again on the set” between lake and river: sediment delivery to the lake → V increases → ℓ rises → sand drops in the backwater → in-channel aggradation of the original route → crevasse → path reorganization.  
　(2) Duty succession is not continuation of the original relation: the original relation (P bears ρ*) is lost after the avulsion; the frozen duty ρ* is borne by another path, N, and between the two lies a transition period registering only “same-kind relation only” and “duty interrupted”. The four-category separate report shows that succession is an adjudicable sequence of events, not “the same law persisting”.  
　(3) The sediment-transport duty transfers before the throughflow duty (X3e): during the transition period N already bears almost all channel-exit sediment transport (by default all routes have the same bar-retention rate, so this equals the actual lake-entering share, 11.4), while its throughflow share has not yet crossed θ. In this model the bottleneck of succession is the water conveyance capacity of the new channel (the initial shoaling of its mouth bar and gully mouth), not sediment transport.  
　(4) Relation to Section 8: the candidate dominance of Section 8 holds under a clamped lake level; when the lake basin can feed back and outflow capacity is low, the “future” of the same candidate dominance is rewritten by the lake-basin state. Dom\_8 of P8 is an adjudication on 𝔐21 under a protocol with constant lake level and cannot be read as dominance under lake-basin feedback. To adjudicate Dom on 𝔐21⁺, a protocol that includes the lake-basin state must be frozen separately (\[pending\], U21-4).  
　(5) Relation to the literature (only to the levels checked in 12.9): between the “no preferential reoccupation” of Ganti 2016 and the “revisiting of old paths” of Reitz 2010, the model supplies only a condition: when a rising lake level makes the original route aggrade and crevasse, the new channel is the shorter path, the old channel heals, and the original route is not reoccupied (in this model N is generated only once and reoccupation is not modeled, U21-3). The increase in avulsion frequency with rising base level described in the abstract of Chadwick 2022 points in the same direction as X4 (the higher the lake level, the earlier the avulsion); agreement in direction is not certification.

### 11.9  Strength and Gaps of This Section

　Column A: Proposition 21-11 and Lemma 21-12 \[proved\] (each within its listed working domain and conditions); the trajectory readings of X0–X5 \[verified\]. The four-category report is a result on one fixed inflow and one starting point, and the outcome is conditional on (ko, z0); N can be generated only once, at a preset location; healing is by diffusion only; lake-outlet outflow is a linear weir (realized outflow capped by available water); these are representational choices. No formal law-formation read-back and no adjudication of dominance under perturbation is made on 𝔐21⁺. Column B: P21-8–P21-10, P21-4b. Column C: none.

12\. Existing Scholarship and Its Relation to This Paper  
The reading level of each source in this section is noted entry by entry (bibliographic record / abstract / main text), and citation goes only to the level read; original figures were not viewed, so no figure numbers are cited; numerical values are taken only from sentences actually read. Each source supports only the propositions it states; “this paper adopts / adds / distinguishes” is this paper's reading, and the sources do not endorse these generalizations. Each entry is arranged as “what is established \| where this paper adopts it \| what this paper adds \| conflict or point of distinction”. Reading levels are listed in 12.9.  
12.1  Channel Initiation and Thresholds of Channel Formation  
　Montgomery and Dietrich 1988 (Nature 336: 232–234; abstract level, main text not obtained): the abstract states that the source area above channel heads decreases with increasing local valley gradient (the abstract read gives a slope range of about 5°–45°), that channel initiation in steep humid terrain is associated with landsliding, contrary to theoretical predictions for initiation by overland flow on gentle valley slopes, and that source areas are larger in arid regions. \| Adopted: channel initiation is an area–slope threshold phenomenon; in Section 4 this paper represents the threshold for “whether a gully mouth can incise” by above-threshold scour (L3) and the gentle footslope (Se). \| Added: the object is not the position of the channel head but under which inflow orders the Trace-Response of an existing old gully mouth at the footslope is amplified or buried (Theorem 21-7), that is, the problem of “historical path selection” above the threshold. \| Distinction: the area–slope threshold says where channels can start; by itself it does not predict which of two paths both above threshold gains dominance, which in this paper depends on order and on old Trace-Responses.  
　Tucker and Slingerland 1997 (Water Resources Research 33(8): 2031–2047; main-text level): the model includes critical shear stresses for grain entrainment and for channel initiation; alluvial channels are transport-limited (a thresholded modified Bagnold sediment-transport formula), bedrock channels are detachment-limited (erosion rate proportional to the excess of shear stress over the threshold), and hillslopes undergo linear diffusion; the abstract states that increased runoff causes rapid expansion of the channel network, that increased sediment supply initially causes aggradation, that the nature and timescale of the response depend on the direction of change, and that the response to decreased runoff is much slower. \| Adopted: the forms of L3 (detachment-limited above-threshold scour), L4 (transport-limited above-threshold sediment transport) and L8 (linear hillslope diffusion) are taken from landscape evolution models of this kind. \| Added: this paper advances “the response depends on the direction of change” to a theorem and an adjudication that “the same cumulative totals in different orders generate different organizations” (Theorem 21-7), and gives an adjudicable definition of a law. \| Distinction: that paper treats the response paths of a drainage basin after climate change; this paper treats how event order within one climate class decides “whether a law is generated”; the two are compatible but different.  
12.2  Landscape Evolution Models and the Stream-Power Law  
　Howard 1994 (Water Resources Research 30: 2261–2285; bibliographic-record level, abstract not obtained): the title names a detachment-limited model of drainage basin evolution. \| Adopted: the “detachment-limited” form of L3 (as a source of the form only; no details of its content are cited).  
　Whipple and Tucker 1999 (JGR 104(B8): 17661–17674; main-text level): the stream-power erosion law E = K A^m S^n (Eq. 1 of the main text); the abstract states that the response time to changes in uplift rate depends on climate, lithology and other factors; the main text states that an erosion threshold term can be added to numerical solutions but is not included in that model. \| Adopted: scour increases with flow rate and slope (in this paper ω = Q\_j·S, a rational form with n = 1), with a threshold added (ωc). \| Distinction: flow rate is not proxied by drainage area A but is actually given event by event by the flow-division law (L2); this is how “the carrying-out of tendency” is concretely borne in this paper.  
　Tucker, Lancaster, Gasparini and Bras 2001 (the CHILD model, Landscape Erosion and Evolution Modeling 349–388; bibliographic-record level); Hobley et al. 2017 (Earth Surface Dynamics 5: 21–46; abstract level: Landlab is an open-source framework for accelerating the development of new process models, providing grids, interoperable process components and coupling interfaces). \| Adopted: the model of this paper is a simplified node–edge model of the same “assembly of process components” kind. \| Added: exact rational arithmetic and an adjudicable definition of dominance, which continuous numerical models do not provide. \| Distinction: the results of this paper are not reproductions of any CHILD/Landlab result.  
12.3  Self-Organization of River Networks and Optimal Channel Networks  
　Rodríguez-Iturbe et al. 1992 (Water Resources Research 28(4): 1095–1103; main-text level): three principles of energy dissipation (minimum energy expenditure in each link of the network, minimum total energy expenditure of the whole network, and equal energy expenditure per unit channel area everywhere) are used to derive the main structural features of drainage networks; the abstract states that their joint application yields a unified picture of the main empirical facts.  
　Rinaldo, Rigon, Banavar, Maritan and Rodríguez-Iturbe 2014, *Evolution and selection of river networks: Statics, dynamics, and complexity* (PNAS 111(7), first page 2417, doi 10.1073/pnas.1322700111; main-text level, read from a PDF in the institutional repository of the University of Trento, with volume, issue and first page confirmed by the ADS bibliographic record): the abstract states that optimal channel networks (OCNs) are feasible optimal configurations of network formation, and that dynamically accessible local optima stabilize into diverse metastable forms; the introduction states that “feasible optimality” means seeking the dynamically accessible optimum under given initial conditions; the main text states that the search is “myopic” and that OCNs carry a long-lasting imprint of initial conditions. \| Adopted: accessible configurations are diverse and carry the imprint of initial conditions and of historical choices; taken as known background. \| Added: in a mechanistic model, this paper gives which segment of history and which Trace-Response determine the configuration reached, and when that configuration gains dominance over subsequent activity (Section 8). \| Distinction: OCN work itself includes multiple accessible stable states and historical choice, so order independence cannot be inferred from “optimality”; only the specific variant “the globally optimal network is unique and determined by the forcing” stands opposed to the order dependence of this paper (12.8 O′).  
12.4  Avulsion on Alluvial Fans and Deltas, Backwater and Flow-Path Memory, Healing of Abandoned Channels  
　Reitz, Jerolmack and Swenson 2010 (GRL 37: L06401; main-text level, full text read via core.ac.uk): flow abruptly concentrates into a single channel; in-channel deposition backfills from the fan edge toward the fan apex, followed by a “searching” phase in which multiple paths fill with water, and after a new path is selected flow concentrates again; “subsequent avulsions consistently returned flow to previously established paths rather than creating new ones”; an “active channel set” of 4 to 5 periodically revisited channels forms; this is modeled as a directed random walk with memory, in which paths not visited for n steps are erased. \| Adopted: old paths act as Trace-Responses that attract later flow (the old gully mouth u\_P of this paper). \| Added: adjudicable conditions for when an old Trace-Response is amplified and when it is buried and erased (the burial erasure of Theorem 21-7(B)). \| Distinction: in that paper memory is written into the model rule as “erased after n steps without a visit”: memory fades only with time without a visit, and filling itself does not change memory; in this paper erasure is derived from the mechanism laws (low flows under divided flow drop all their sand at the footslope, burying down to the burial limit), and filling does change memory. The two give different predictions under a protocol of “filling while the time has not yet elapsed” (P21-2).  
　Ganti, Chadwick, Hassenruck-Gudipati and Lamb 2016 (JGR Earth Surface 121; main-text level, up to the reference list): “32 avulsions (78%) occurred during high-flow events”; the avulsion threshold is “approximately 0.3hc in our experiments”; at low flow the spatial deceleration of flow causes deposition in the upstream part of the backwater zone, while at high flow scour starts near the shoreline; the conclusions state that there is “no evidence for preferential reoccupation of flow paths from previous avulsion cycles”. \| Adopted: deposition in the backwater zone and mouth bars raising the base level make channel reaches drop sand (L5), with different write-backs at high and low flow. \| Added: the exact critical mouth-bar height m\* for backwater aggradation of the channel reach (Theorem 21-5(B)) and “if negative write-back prevails, the law is not generated” (Theorem 21-8(C)). \| Distinction: Ganti 2016 does not support preferential reoccupation, while Reitz 2010 does. This paper gives a condition that can accommodate both outcomes: when backwater and mouth-bar negative write-back are strong, the old path does not gain dominance (Theorem 21-8(C)); under slope control with weak negative write-back, the old path is amplified (Theorem 21-7(A)). This is an explanation to be tested, not a ruling between the two papers.  
　Chadwick, Steele, Silvestre and Lamb 2022 (JGR Earth Surface 127(7): e2021JF006422; abstract level): rivers avulse more frequently when sea-level rise accelerates; rivers avulse repeatedly where and when they have aggraded to nearly half a channel depth, forming preferential avulsion nodes within the backwater zone that do not shift with sea-level change. \| Adopted: base level (lake level) as an external driver that changes sand deposition in the backwater (ℓ in this paper). \| Added: when a high flood-season lake level is in phase with inflow, the law forms temporarily and is then lost (Theorem 21-8(C)).  
　Ganti, Chu, Lamb, Nittrouer and Parker 2014 (GRL 41: 7882–7890; main-text level): avulsions on fans occur where bed slope decreases abruptly, and delta avulsions cluster at locations affected by backwater hydrodynamics; the avulsion frequency at fan nodes matches the channel-filling timescale set by levee superelevation. \| Adopted: the gentle footslope (Se taken smaller than upstream) as the declared basis for deposition and avulsion being likely there.  
　Martin and Edmonds 2022 (Earth Surface Dynamics 10: 555–579; abstract level, DOAJ page): stronger repulsion by abandoned channels moves avulsions away from the mountain front, and stronger attraction moves them toward the source; of three healing scenarios only “healing toward the far field” reaches dynamic equilibrium without filling the accommodation space. \| Adopted: abandoned Trace-Responses can both attract and repel (in this paper the footslope deposit of the opposite route is a repelling Trace-Response, the old gully mouth an attracting one). \| Distinction: in that paper attraction and repulsion are rules written into the model, and healing (filling) can change memory; in this paper they are derived from the flow-division and sand-deposition laws. Healing-type memory models agree with this paper on “filling changes memory”, so a distinction must be set up separately (for example, the interaction between healing rate and inflow order, \[pending\]).  
　Paola, Straub, Mohrig and Reinhardt 2009 (Earth-Science Reviews, pp. 1–43; abstract level, volume number not given on the page read): the “unreasonable effectiveness” of experiments in stratigraphy and geomorphology comes from the scale independence of nature; autogenic dynamics produce most of the spatial structure in landscapes and in preserved stratigraphy. \| Adopted: as the basis for experimental fans and flume deltas being the preferred test platforms for Column B of this paper (Section 13).  
12.5  River-Mouth Bars, Blockage, and the Triggering Conditions and Superelevation Thresholds of Crevassing  
　Edmonds and Slingerland 2007 (JGR 112: F02034; main-text level: abstract, Sections 5.4 and 5.5, and conclusions): the abstract states that the distance to the mouth bar is proportional to the jet momentum flux and inversely proportional to grain size; the conclusions state that when the water depth over the bar is equal to or less than 40% of the inlet depth, the mouth bar stops prograding, and fluid pressure on the upstream side of the bar is enough to make the flow split around the bar. \| Adopted: the mouth bar as a lakeshore blocking structure that raises the outlet sill height of its own route and diverts flow around it (the role of m\_j in L2). \| Distinction: mouth bars in this paper do not split the channel (the two routes are fixed), so new branches induced by mouth bars are not covered (open, U21-3).  
　Slingerland and Smith 2004 (Annual Review of Earth and Planetary Sciences 32: 257–285; main-text level): a high aggradation rate of the main channel is the key process that widens the difference in conveyance capacity between the two branches; superelevation is a normalized quantity (e.g., the height of the levee above the floodplain divided by the depth of the parent channel); the water-surface slope advantage of the crevasse channel over the parent channel is the core of avulsion; in stability analysis, multiple stable flow solutions appear once a threshold is reached as the Shields number decreases; the trigger is an event that abruptly changes the conveyance capacity of the channel (floods, logjams, bank failures). \| Adopted: the dichotomy of “setup and trigger” corresponds to this paper's “slow writing of Trace-Responses” and “event beats”; the perturbation classes (bank failure, bank; sand added to the mouth bar, bar) are taken from such triggers.  
　Bolla Pittaluga, Repetto and Tubino 2003 (equilibrium configurations and stability of channel bifurcations; IRIS bibliographic-record and abstract level, journal volume and pages not given on the pages read): the nodal point condition plays a decisive role in one-dimensional models (citing Wang et al. 1995); when the upstream Shields number is large enough there is only a single stable solution with both branches open, and as the Shields number decreases two stable solutions with different flow partitions appear and the original solution becomes unstable. \| Adopted: uneven partition at a bifurcation is multistability below a threshold; this is the known physical premise for “a law can be generated”. \| Added: beyond the existence of multistability, Theorem 21-7 gives which segment of history sends the system into which branch.  
　(The main text or abstract of Jerolmack and Mohrig 2007, *Conditions for branching in depositional rivers*, and of Mohrig et al. 2000 was not read; their content is not cited.)  
12.6  Historical Dependence, Hysteresis, Multistability and Path Dependence in Geomorphology; Signal Shredding and Climatic Order
